Hiresense  ·  Apollo Knowledge Services

Apollo Knowledge GWD — AI-Native Healthcare Talent Platform

Six-month delivery plan. Sixteen milestones covering the platform foundations, the client's Release 1 list and the Release 2 items deferred from it, worked to item level for design and engineering. Effort, risk and open decisions are shown per item.
v0.48 August 2026 Sources: Part A v2 · Part B M3 v1 16 milestones · 208 items · 1062 conditions 1362 person-days

How to read this

This plan covers the whole six-month engagement in one document. It is written to be reviewed by the client product manager and picked up by the UX team, and the milestone detail is written plainly enough for a developer joining cold.

Why this is not split into Release 1 and Release 2. The engagement was scoped as two releases across six months. At revision the client moved nine Release 2 deliverables into Release 1 and added fourteen new ones, so Release 1 alone is now thirty-four deliverables. A three-month-plus-three-month split no longer describes the work. This plan therefore treats the six months as one delivery with fifteen milestones, sequenced by what depends on what. Milestones M10 to M15 are the items still sitting on the client's Release 2 list and are marked as such throughout.

The engineering index

Every screen, service and integration has one identifier of the form M<milestone>-I<number>. This is the reference used in wireframes, tickets, test cases and conversations. It maps to the client's own deliverable numbering, which is shown alongside it, so either side can talk in their own terms without ambiguity.

IdentifierWhat it isExample
M<n>-I<k>The engineering index. One per buildable item. Use this everywhere.M3-I12 — Fulfilment board
M<n>Milestone. M1 to M9 are the client's Release 1 list; M10 to M15 are the Release 2 list.M3 — Demand Intake & Allocation
D<n> / A<n>The client's own deliverable number, carried unchanged. Shown on every item.D2, A1, A13
F<nn>A platform prerequisite Hiresense identified. Not in the client's list.F03 — Role & permission model
OI-…An open decision. Source is the client's Part A, Part B, or Hiresense.OI-A-12, OI-HS-01
Linked itemsOther items this one touches. The link is not directional — it may be something this item needs, or something that consumes it. Build order is stated at milestone level, on the Sequencing tab.M3-I17 links to M9-I9
AS-<nn>An assumption we are working to until an open item closes.AS-04

Scope discipline

Every element and scenario listed against an item is required to release that item. Anything that would help but does not gate a release has been taken out, so the estimate reflects the minimum rather than the comfortable version. Five classes of thing were removed:

Settings we can fix insteadWhere a single sensible value works, it is fixed in the build rather than exposed as a setting. Audit retention is a fixed policy, not a configuration screen. Only the settings the client explicitly asked to vary per client stay configurable.
Duplication of shared componentsExport, saved views and column choosers appear once in M0-I7 and are inherited by every list. Listing them per screen inflated the estimate without adding work.
Measurement inside operating screensParse accuracy, override rates, suggestion quality and usage counts have been removed from the screens where the work happens. Reporting lives in M9; instrumenting every screen separately is a second reporting layer nobody asked for.
Comparison, preview and trend viewsSide-by-side diffs, period-on-period comparisons, sample previews and trend lines. Useful, and none of them stops a release.
Bulk and shortcut actionsRemoved unless the client named them — bulk salary revision stays because D23 asks for it; bulk reclassification of inbound messages goes because nothing asks for it.
Anything the client did not stateEach screen traces to something the client wrote. Where they asked for an outcome, the plan carries the smallest screen that achieves it and nothing more. Where they were silent, there is no screen — the gap is recorded as an open item instead of being filled with a guess.
If something here looks thin, that is deliberate. Where a convenience was cut and you disagree, it is a conversation about adding scope back with a number attached, not a gap in the analysis. Anything the client document names explicitly has been kept, even where it looked optional.

Effort

Every item carries an Effort Band — a readable size that maps to a fixed number of person-days, so bands add up into a timeline. The band covers build. Design and QA are shown separately because they are done by different people at different points.

BandPerson-daysWhat it means
XS0.5Trivial. Config toggle, static panel, single read-only view. AI scaffolds it almost entirely.
S1.0Simple CRUD list or form against a settled schema. Low branching.
M2.0Form or view with conditional logic, several states, or moderate server logic.
L3.0Multi-state screen with tabs, or a domain service with a real state machine.
XL5.0Subsystem component, or integration against a documented and stable external API.
XXL8.0Integration with an unconfirmed or unstable external, data migration, or a model/accuracy-dependent component.

AI leverage

The effort figures assume AI-assisted development. That help is not even across the work — it removes most of the effort on a standard form and almost none on an integration with a supplier nobody has chosen yet. Stating it per item is what makes the estimate defensible.

High HighAI-assisted development removes most of the work. Scaffolded CRUD, forms, list views, standard validation, generated tests. Estimate assumes 60-70% acceleration against manual coding.
Medium MediumAI accelerates the first draft but review, integration wiring and edge cases stay human. Assumes 30-40% acceleration.
Low LowAI gives little leverage. External API debugging, unstable third-party behaviour, migration against unseen data, model tuning, or spec ambiguity that no amount of code generation resolves. Assumes 0-15% acceleration.

Risk

Risk is about difficulty and exposure, not importance. A big screen with a clear spec is low risk; a small integration with an unnamed supplier is high. The driver code says why.

LevelMeaning
LowSelf-contained, spec settled, no external party. Slippage affects this item only.
MediumCross-module contract, shared component, or a spec gap with a workable assumption in place.
HighUnconfirmed external dependency, unresolved open item blocking build, accuracy-dependent behaviour, or a component several modules break without.
CodeDriverMeaning
EXTExternal dependencyThird-party API, provider not yet named, or behaviour outside our control.
SPECUnresolved specificationAn open item must close before this can be built correctly.
SHAREDShared componentBuilt once, consumed by several modules. A defect or delay multiplies.
DATAData volume or migrationDepends on data we have not yet profiled.
MODELAccuracy-dependentParsing, transcription or classification quality determines whether it works at all.
COMPComplianceDPDP, audit or retention obligations attach.
XMODCross-module contractA schema two modules must agree before either is built.

Confidence

High — the specification is complete. Medium — an assumption covers a gap that is unlikely to change the shape of the work. Low — an open decision could change what gets built, not just how much. 97 of 208 items are Low, and each one names the open item responsible. Every M10 to M15 item is Low, because no client specification exists for them yet.

Where the effort sits. M0 foundations plus the client Release 1 list comes to 1148 person-days. The Release 2 list comes to 214. That ratio is the point: because nine deliverables were pulled forward and fourteen added, roughly 84% of the six-month effort now sits in the foundations plus what is still labelled Release 1.

What is blocking a committable estimate

7 of the 49 open items have to close before a fixed price can be put on this work. Each one names the exact items it holds up, by engineering index, so the effect is countable rather than general.

OI-M3-03How many client HR or recruitment systems must we connect to?3 items held22.0pd affectedPart B M3 #3
A1 lists 'client HRMS/ATS' as one of the five ways a vacancy can reach the platform. Some clients will push vacancies straight from their own system rather than emailing them. Every one of those systems is a separate connector with its own payload shape, its own credentials and its own test data - so 'two clients' and 'nine clients' are completely different pieces of work. Nobody has said which client systems are in scope or how many.

Items it holds up

  • M3-I26 — Client HR system feed (M3, 11.0pd, integration)
  • M3-I20 — Parsers (M3, 7.5pd, service)
  • M3-I5 — Channel settings (M3, 3.5pd, config)

Why it blocks the estimate

The connector framework can be built without the answer, but the number of connectors cannot be priced. Quoting a fixed price now means guessing how many client systems Apollo will name later.

Who decides

Suman, Head of Enterprise Partnerships India — needed before the A1 estimate is finalised.

OI-M3-04How many different client spreadsheet layouts must we read?2 items held16.5pd affectedPart B M3 #4
Several clients send their vacancies as a spreadsheet in their own format - different column names, different order, sometimes several vacancies per file. We build one mapping screen, but each client format then needs its own mapping set up and its own sample file to test against. The number of formats drives the effort; nobody has stated it.

Items it holds up

  • M3-I6 — Spreadsheet mapping (M3, 9.0pd, screen)
  • M3-I20 — Parsers (M3, 7.5pd, service)

Why it blocks the estimate

Same exposure as OI-M3-03. The mapping screen is priceable; the per-client mapping work is not until the count is known.

Who decides

Shallu Kakkar, Head of Global Fulfillment — needed before the A1 estimate is finalised.

OI-A-02How much data is in CPAL, and what condition is it in?6 items held31.0pd affectedPart A M1
A10 moves Apollo's existing candidate data out of the old tool and into the platform, after which the platform becomes the system of record and the old tool is switched off. We have never seen this data. We do not know how many records there are, how complete they are, how many duplicates it contains, or whether fields map cleanly onto the new model. Part A says a joint profiling exercise will settle this, and Apollo will provide access. It has not happened yet.

Items it holds up

  • M1-I10 — CPAL data profiling (M1, 6.0pd, service)
  • M1-I11 — Migration pipeline (M1, 11.0pd, service)
  • M1-I12 — Duplicate resolution on migrated data (M1, 8.0pd, service)
  • M1-I2 — Migration screens (M1, 6.0pd, screen)
  • M1-I2 — Migration screens (M1, 6.0pd, screen)
  • M1-I2 — Migration screens (M1, 6.0pd, screen)

Why it blocks the estimate

A10 cannot be sized at all. The figures shown against these six items are placeholders to hold the shape of the plan, not estimates. This is the single largest unpriced item in the six months.

Who decides

Apollo (provides access), Hiresense (runs the profiling) — needed before any estimate is committed.

OI-A-09Which provider will make the automated phone calls?5 items held44.0pd affectedPart A M5
A5 calls nurses who have not responded on any other channel, asks whether they are still available, and writes the answer back. Apollo buys the calling service; we build the integration and the part that turns a spoken answer into structured data. Different providers differ enormously in how conversations are handled, what the transcript looks like, and which Indian languages are covered. Apollo has not named one.

Items it holds up

  • M5-I12 — Voice provider (M5, 11.0pd, integration)
  • M5-I13 — Voice answer extraction (M5, 7.5pd, service)
  • M5-I6 — Voice campaigns (M5, 6.0pd, config)
  • M5-I7 — Replies to review (M5, 8.5pd, screen)
  • M3-I23 — Voice note transcription (M3, 11.0pd, integration)

Why it blocks the estimate

The integration cannot be sized against an unknown API, and language coverage cannot be tested. It also affects M3: if the same provider does speech-to-text for inbound voice notes we build one integration, otherwise two.

Who decides

Apollo — needed before the M5 estimate is committed.

OI-A-13How long is the guarantee period, and does it run past day 90?4 items held28.5pd affectedPart A M9
Apollo now invoices a permanent placement once the client confirms at day 90 that the person is still there. That timing removes the need to reverse an invoice if someone leaves early. But if the contract guarantees a replacement for longer than 90 days - six months, say - then a candidate leaving in month five means an invoice already raised has to be corrected. That requires credit and debit notes, which are currently out of scope until M15.

Items it holds up

  • M9-I12 — Permanent invoice (M9, 7.5pd, service)
  • M9-I1 — Invoices (M9, 6.0pd, screen)
  • M2-I8 — Contract details (M2, 9.0pd, screen)
  • M15-I5 — Credit and debit notes (M15, 6.0pd, screen)

Why it blocks the estimate

This changes scope, not just a number. If the guarantee runs past day 90, credit and debit notes move from M15 into the earlier billing work and the contract screen has to capture the period.

Who decides

Apollo — needed before any estimate is committed.

OI-HS-05How many messages will the platform actually send per month?4 items held30.5pd affectedHiresense
The client's own Part B asks us for this. WhatsApp is billed per conversation, transcription per minute and automated calls per minute, so running cost depends on volume rather than on the build. The trouble is that volume is set by the contact schedule - the shift pool is contacted every three to four days across the whole base - and that schedule is configuration nobody has settled yet. So the question runs both ways: Apollo needs a cost figure from us, and we need their expected base size and contact frequency to produce one.

Items it holds up

  • M3-I24 — WhatsApp inbound and follow-up (M3, 7.5pd, integration)
  • M5-I8 — Message sender (M5, 7.0pd, service)
  • M5-I3 — Delivery log (M5, 5.5pd, screen)
  • M5-I5 — Contact schedule (M5, 10.5pd, config)

Why it blocks the estimate

Running cost cannot be modelled, which is a commercial exposure rather than a build one. If the shift pool is larger than assumed, per-conversation cost could exceed the build cost of the module that sends the messages.

Who decides

Shallu Kakkar with Hiresense — needed before the estimate response goes back.

OI-HS-07Who supplies document reading, electronic signature and WhatsApp?4 items held27.5pd affectedHiresense (M4/M7)
Three separate suppliers are needed and neither client document names any of them. Document OCR reads registration certificates and ID documents so the verification team is not retyping them. Electronic signature gets offers signed. The WhatsApp Business Solution Provider carries every message the platform sends to candidates and clients, and also has to approve each message template before it can be used - which takes weeks and is outside our control.

Items it holds up

  • M4-I23 — Document text extraction (M4, 7.0pd, integration)
  • M7-I15 — Electronic signature (M7, 7.0pd, integration)
  • M3-I24 — WhatsApp inbound and follow-up (M3, 7.5pd, integration)
  • M5-I1 — Message templates (M5, 6.0pd, screen)

Why it blocks the estimate

Three integrations cannot be sized against unknown APIs. The WhatsApp one is worse than an estimate problem: template approval is a calendar dependency that no amount of team size removes, and it gates the whole contact schedule going live in M5.

Who decides

Tamajit Mukherjee with Mr. Sriram, PMO — needed before M4 and M7 build starts.

Scope divergence with the signed SOW. The client's current Release 1 is not the Release 1 the signed functional SOW describes. Candidate scope is now nurses, doctors and allied health rather than nurses only, and the client portal is deferred so there is no client login anywhere until M10. Until the client's deliverables are mapped against the 24 capability blocks in the signed SOW (OI-HS-03), the two documents describe different projects.

How the work is delivered

PrincipleWhat it means
A working path before a thick oneEach category is taken end to end - one client, one requisition, one candidate sourced, ranked, shortlisted, placed, tracked to day 90 and invoiced - before any part of it is thickened. Nurses first, then doctors, then allied health. Module coherence remains a secondary aim because it is cheaper to build, but it gives way to getting a path working.
Gates every three to four weeksEach gate is a working demonstration rather than a document. Named Apollo attendees: Shallu Kakkar for fulfilment and the recruiter workflow, Suman for the client-facing surfaces, Mr. Abednego Peter for clinical quality and the candidate record. A written record at each gate of what was raised and what changed.
Sign-off on what can be operatedAcceptance is tied to a gate producing something a stakeholder can use, not to a milestone being complete.
The scenario catalogue is authored jointlyThe conditions each item must satisfy are settled gate by gate rather than fixed during design. Where feedback at a gate concerns capability already inside the agreed scope, it completes the specification for that capability. Where it introduces capability the plan does not describe, it is a change.
Three assumptions the gates exist to settleWhether recruiters accept a ranked shortlist and what their overrides imply about the weighting; whether a hospital contact will click a link to confirm retention at day 90; and whether candidates install the app, since shift attendance has no other route.

Basis of the estimate

ItemBasis
Working daysA month is taken as 21 working days. Six months is 126 working days per person.
Effort to calendarPerson-days divide by team size, then get checked against the build order and the couplings. Several milestones cannot run at the same time however many people are on them.
What is not in the figuresDiscovery workshops, client sessions, defect cycles past first-pass QA, project management, and the 500 consulting hours in the legal SOW. UAT support is in M1 as a line item; the rest is not.
ConfidenceMilestones M1 to M9 are sized from the client's Release 1 list. M10 to M15 are sized from capability names only, with no client chapter, and all their items carry Low confidence.
On the team size figure. Total effort divided by 126 working days gives the average team size needed to finish inside six months. That average is not a plan on its own: design front-loads into months one and two, QA back-loads into months five and six, and the integration-heavy items in M1, M4, M5 and M13 need specialists rather than more generalists. Treat the number as the floor for capacity planning, then shape the team around the build order.
The back half is compressed, and this is the main schedule risk. Five of the ten build steps begin in months five and six: M8, then M9 and M11, then M10, M12 and M14, then M15, then M13. Each of those steps needs the one before it, so they cannot simply be run in parallel by adding people. Two consequences worth stating now. First, any slip in months one to four lands entirely on the Release 2 milestones, because they are the only things left to cut. Second, M13 payroll sits last behind three other milestones — if the contractual line matters commercially, it is the item most exposed to everything upstream of it.

Deliverables

Every deliverable across the six months, keyed by our own index. The client's deliverable number is carried alongside it, and the right-hand column lists the exact items that implement it, so a deliverable can always be traced down to buildable work. Deliverables that arrived as a thin slice in one milestone and a remainder in another appear twice, once for each. D15 and D17 were descoped from the contract entirely and do not appear.

IndexClient refDeliverableReleaseProvenancePriSurfaceRiskImplemented byScope note
M0F01Login and session handlingFoundationsHiresense-definedP0AllMedium COMPM0-I1How an Apollo user gets into the platform and stays in. D11 asks for role-based access but never says how anyone signs in.
M0F02User managementFoundationsHiresense-definedP0AllLow SHAREDM0-I2Creating and managing the Apollo staff who use the platform. M3 requires a requisition owner from 'the Apollo internal user list' - nothing else creates that list.
M0F03Roles and permissionsFoundationsHiresense-definedP0AllHigh SPECM0-I3What each role can see and do. D11 says 'role-based access control' as a phrase - the actual permission model, and whether a recruiter sees only their own site, is not specified anywhere.
M0F04Audit trailFoundationsHiresense-definedP0AllMedium COMPM0-I4One place that records who did what and when. Several milestones need this - M3 status changes, M7 selection decisions, M7 link responses - and building it six times would be six different answers.
M0F05Settings and lists administrationFoundationsHiresense-definedP0AllMedium SHAREDM0-I5One admin area for every configurable list and waiting period in the platform. There are at least nine of them; without a shared home they become nine separate screens.
M0F06Document and file storageFoundationsHiresense-definedP0AllMedium COMPM0-I6Where every uploaded file lives: CVs, certificates, ID scans, WhatsApp voice notes, signed offers, invoices.
M3F07Message sending infrastructureRelease 1Hiresense-definedP0AllHigh EXTM3-I30The plumbing underneath every message the platform sends. D10 covers templates and preferences; the queue, retries and delivery receipts underneath are assumed.
M2F08Lists, search, filters and exportRelease 1Hiresense-definedP0AllLow SHAREDM2-I14The shared table component every Ops screen uses. Around twenty screens in this plan are lists; building filtering and paging separately in each is the same work twenty times.
M1F10Environments, pipeline and monitoringRelease 1Hiresense-definedP0AllMedium EXTM1-I16Four environments, a way to move code between them, and enough monitoring to know when something has broken.
M1F12Design system and component libraryRelease 1Hiresense-definedP0AllLow SHAREDM1-I4The shared visual language and the components built from it. Five different surfaces in this plan - if each is designed separately the work is done five times and looks like it.
M4F13Data protection: consent, residency, retentionRelease 1Hiresense-definedP0AllHigh COMPM4-I27Handling personal and health-related data about candidates in a way that meets Indian data protection law.
M3F14Event recording for reportingRelease 1Hiresense-definedP0AllHigh DATAM3-I31Every module writes an event as things happen, and the reports in M9 read those events later. This is the one prerequisite that cannot be added afterwards.
M3D2Demand managementRelease 1Original R1P0Ops/AdminMedium SPECM3-I9, M3-I10, M3-I11, M3-I12, M3-I13, M3-I14, M3-I15, M3-I27, M3-I28Requisition object and lifecycle: creation, versioning, hold, revise, close; shift templates; fulfilment board and aging.
M3A1Multi-channel demand ingestionRelease 1New additionP0Ops/AdminHigh EXTM3-I1, M3-I2, M3-I3, M3-I4, M3-I5, M3-I6, M3-I7, M3-I8, M3-I17, M3-I18, M3-I19, M3-I20, M3-I21, M3-I22, M3-I23, M3-I24, M3-I25, M3-I26, M3-I29, M3-I32Five live channels plus verbal capture, all normalising to one requisition schema. Owns the inbound stack consumed by A14.

M1D11Foundations & documentationRelease 1Original R1P0Ops/AdminHigh SHAREDM1-I1, M1-I5, M1-I6, M1-I7, M1-I8, M1-I9Deployment to the client-hosted AWS Mumbai account, technical and user documentation, UAT support and go-live. Access control, environments and audit are delivered once in M0 rather than separately here.

Module structure

Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure
M1 — Platform Foundations & Migration

Carries role-based access control, deployment to the client-hosted AWS Mumbai environment, technical and user documentation, UAT support and go-live for the enlarged Release 1, together with the market adapter interfaces sitting behind identity, certification and qualification. It also carries migration of CPAL and current tooling into the platform as the system of record, including retirement of the legacy tool.

Deliverables: D11, A10, A12

Surfaces: Ops/Admin only — administration and configuration screens. No candidate-facing or client-facing surface.

OPEN — A12 market coverage. Only the India adapter is in scope for Release 1. With three candidate categories, the India adapter set now spans nursing councils, the NMC and state medical councils, the allied health councils and the Pharmacy Council; several have no API and resolve to document plus manual verification behind the same interface. The requirement is that the interface accepts the US, Germany and Ireland later without change to core domain logic. The set of interface contracts to be proven against those three markets in Release 1 is not enumerated in the revision.
OPEN — A10 effort basis. Estimation is to follow a joint data profiling exercise that has not yet run. Apollo will provide access for profiling ahead of estimation.
OPEN — A10 against D3 duplicate handling. D3 scopes duplicate detection and merge for app self-registration. The revision does not state whether the migrated CPAL base is resolved through the same mechanism or through a one-time migration-side process.

Note: the LMS is being built by an internal Apollo team and is outside HireSense scope. It is expected to connect to this platform via API at a later point; no Release 1 deliverable depends on it and no LMS integration is scoped here.

M2 — Client, Contract & Rate Card

Holds the client entity with its locations and departments, rate cards by role, skill and location, and the contract record with versioning, renewal alerts and history. It supplies the contract terms the billing triggers read — the day-90 retention terms for permanent, and the timesheet approval terms for contractual — including the per-client windows after which retention is deemed confirmed and a timesheet deemed approved where a client does not respond.

Deliverables: D1, D13

Surfaces: Ops/Admin.

OPEN — D1 user provisioning scope. D1 includes user provisioning with role-based access. D12 (client portal) is deferred and A13 operates without login or account provisioning, so there is no client-side login in Release 1. Confirm that D1 provisioning in Release 1 covers Apollo internal users only.
CLOSED — Deemed-confirmation and deemed-approval windows. Both are configurable per client and held in D13: the day-90 retention deemed-confirmation window, and the timesheet deemed-approval window added with client timesheet approval. Estimate the configurability, not a fixed period. Both require a platform-level default so that a client onboarded without a configured window does not stall invoicing. Apollo has not yet supplied the default values.
M3 — Demand Intake & Allocation

Captures a requisition from any channel the client already uses and normalises it to a single structured requisition object carrying candidate category, with source retained, completeness scored against required fields and follow-up routed back through the originating channel; it also holds shift templates, live fulfilment status and aging, and the demand lifecycle of hold, revise and close. It attaches an owner to the requisition and records the recruiter allocation that the daily morning call already makes.

Deliverables: D2, A1, D14 (thin slice)

Surfaces: Ops/Admin. Email, client-format spreadsheets, WhatsApp text and voice notes and client HRMS/ATS are ingestion paths rather than rendered surfaces; verbal capture by a site-resident recruiter is entered through Ops/Admin.

OPEN — A1 channel count in Release 1. A1 names six ingestion channels including the client portal. D12 is deferred, so five are available in Release 1. Confirm the portal channel is specified but not built, rather than dropped from A1.
OPEN — A9 requisition-side fields. A9 now carries a per-category attribute set on both the requisition and the candidate record, so requisition granularity differs by category. The requisition-side fields are not explicitly scoped into D2. Ownership of that half of A9 needs assigning between M3 and M4.

Scope boundary: the prioritisation engine and the requisition approval workflow remain in the later release. What sits in Release 1 is an owner field and capture of an allocation made outside the system — a record, not an assignment engine. The inbound parsing pipeline is built here under A1 and consumed by A14 in M5.

M4 — Candidate Record & Credentials

Holds the candidate as a record spanning two supply bases against a shared credential object carrying issuer, validity period and verification method, with document OCR, identity verification behind the M1 adapter layer, and a verification queue with pendency tracking. It covers three candidate categories — nurses, doctors and allied health — with A9 held as a per-category attribute set, and includes the candidate mobile app: self-registration with phone and OTP, resume upload and parsing, duplicate detection and merge, job and shift discovery, application and shift tracking, and shift check-in.

Deliverables: D3, D4, D16, A2, A9

Surfaces: Ops/Admin (candidate record, verification queue, pendency tracker) and Candidate App.

OPEN — Clinical and communication rating field. D17 is descoped, but the candidate record retains a clinical and communication rating attribute. No producing system is specified, and whether D18 ranking weights that attribute is not stated. Until the production method is decided, the field is present and unpopulated.
CLOSED — Candidate categories. Resolved. Release 1 covers nurses, doctors and allied health. Category is a field on the requisition and the candidate record; A9 becomes a per-category attribute set — specialty and acuity for nurses, specialty, case-mix and grade for doctors, discipline, equipment and technique competency for allied health. The D15 taxonomy is not revived as a deliverable; it is absorbed into A9. The credential model in D16 is unchanged, so additional registries resolve as adapters and configuration.

D3 is a three-way shared build and a hard dependency. D3 sits in M4, but shift discovery, acceptance and check-in serve M8, and its response surface is one of the two A14 channels in M5. Because shift attendance is captured by app check-in with no alternative route, D3 is a hard dependency of the contractual invoice chain — no app, no attendance, no shift invoice. Sequence it against all three consumers.

M5 — Engagement & Availability

Determines which candidates to contact, on what schedule, through which channel and with which question set, configurable per segment, and delivers on that decision over WhatsApp, SMS, email, app push and outbound voice with template management, trigger configuration, delivery status, retry, opt-out, preference management and a per-user notification log. It captures the response on a single structured schema across the WhatsApp interactive template and the candidate app, writes it back to the candidate record, and resets the availability confidence value that otherwise decays on a configurable schedule per base.

Deliverables: D10, D29, A3, A4, A5, A14

Surfaces: Ops/Admin (cadence configuration, template management, notification and response logs), Candidate App, WhatsApp interactive templates, outbound voice.

A3, A4, A5 and A14 form a closed loop: the cadence engine selects on confidence, delivery goes out, the response returns on a structured schema, and the confirmed response resets confidence, which determines the next selection. None of the four is independently operable. Treat them as one unit for estimation and sequencing — see A5.

Base B cadence runs across the whole base every three to four days, and Base A on a longer cycle; the cadence is configurable per segment, so per-channel message volumes are a function of configuration rather than a fixed number.

CLOSED — Ownership of the shared inbound pipeline. Assigned. The channel receiver, queue, sender identity resolution, parser dispatch, intent classification, unparseable-message handling and retry are built once under A1 in M3. A14 consumes that stack with a candidate-response intent schema. HireSense returns three estimate lines: the pipeline once, A1 schema and routing, A14 schema and routing.
OPEN — Free-text fallback. A14 states that free-text responses fall back to parsing. No accuracy threshold, confidence handling or human-review path is specified for parsed responses that then reset availability confidence in A3.
OPEN — A5 provider. Apollo will confirm the outbound voice provider. The items to be estimated are the integration and the response extraction schema, not the voice service itself.
M6 — Matching & Ranking

Produces a ranked candidate set against a requisition, exposing the rationale for each rank together with unmet or partially met criteria, applying different weightings per candidate category and supply base, with saved searches, talent segments, advanced search on skill, location, experience and status, and recruiter override. It consumes selection outcomes and structured rejection reasons written back from M7 so that subsequent ranking reflects them.

Deliverables: D5, D18, A8

Surfaces: Ops/Admin.

OPEN — Manual shortlist assembly. D5 is described as retaining search and submission while manual shortlist building is superseded by ranked output. Whether hand-assembly survives as a recruiter fallback, or whether recruiter override on the ranked set is the only manual path, is not stated.
OPEN — Ranking input weighting. D18 consumes the credential record (D16), specialty and acuity (A9), availability confidence and recency (A3), and written-back outcomes (A8). With three candidate categories and two supply bases, the weight sets multiply accordingly, and the inputs themselves differ by category. The relative weighting model across those inputs, and its configurability, is not specified.
M7 — Selection, Offer & Client Response

Runs slot proposal and confirmation, reschedule and cancellation, feedback capture, the accept / hold / reject decision with mandatory structured rejection reasons against a configurable taxonomy and a selection audit trail, then generates, delivers, tracks and expires the offer through to digital signature and offer-to-contract mapping. It also provides the tokenised client link mechanism, built once and instantiated by configurable link type — shortlist response, trial check-in at day 30, 60 and 90, and timesheet approval — reached without login or account provisioning and delivered through the channel the client already uses.

Deliverables: D6, D20, A13

Surfaces: Ops/Admin, Client link surfaces, and WhatsApp/email as the delivery channel for the offer and for both links.

CLOSED — A13 scope and ownership. A13 remains one deliverable in M7. The link mechanism is built once and instantiated by type; instances serve M7 (shortlist), M8 (day 30, 60 and 90 check-in) and M9 (timesheet approval). Only the day-90 retention instance is a billing trigger — day 30 and day 60 capture signal and must not create a second trigger path. Price the mechanism once and each type as an instance.
CLOSED — Day-60 client intent. Resolved by the day 30 / 60 / 90 check-in link type. No Ops/Admin workaround is required.
OPEN — Recipient identity model. A13 requires the retention confirmation to be timestamped against an identified recipient. Whether recipients are held per client, per location, per requisition or per placement, and how they are maintained without account provisioning, is not specified.
M8 — Placement, Trial & Shift Attendance

Records joining confirmation with date tracking and drop-out capture with reasons, then tracks a configurable set of check-in events across day 0, 7, 30, 60 and 90 capturing attendance, salary payment timeliness, preceptor assignment and accommodation status, with client intent captured at day 60 and an auditable client-acknowledged record at day 90. It also carries shift-level attendance captured by check-in on the candidate app, and the timesheet, which now holds an approval state set by the client through an A13 link; leave categorisation, overtime treatment and the internal approval routing workflow remain in the later release.

Deliverables: D7, A6, D22 (thin slice)

Surfaces: Ops/Admin (trial dashboard, attendance and timesheet), Candidate App (shift check-in and candidate-side responses), outbound voice/WhatsApp for candidate-side check-ins, Client link surfaces for the day 30, 60 and 90 records and for timesheet approval.

CLOSED — Shift attendance capture mechanism. Attendance is captured by check-in on the candidate app, replacing the Excel import in the original D22. Release 1 records a location stamp against each check-in without enforcing a boundary; geo-fencing moves off the exclusion list to a named Phase 2 item so the field exists when enforcement is specified. Pilot runs on in-house nurses.
CLOSED — Client timesheet approval. Added. The timesheet carries an approval state set by the client through an A13 link type — the same mechanism as the day-90 confirmation, second instance. D25 invoices against an approved timesheet rather than raw attendance. A deemed-approval window is configurable per client in D13, with a platform-level default so an unconfigured client does not stall invoicing.
M9 — Billing, Revenue & Reporting

Generates the permanent placement invoice on the day-90 client-acknowledged retention record rather than at placement, holding that acknowledgement as an auditable trigger record capable of supporting a billing query, and generates shift-level invoices from the client-approved timesheet and contract for the contractual scope. It reports funnel and conversion, fill rate, time-to-fill, trial survival, stage-level conversion loss by client and by recruiter, channel and recruiter performance, the monthly MIS pack, and rate agreed against baseline by client, role, specialty and acuity.

Deliverables: D8, A7, D25 (thin slice), D9, D28, A11

Surfaces: Ops/Admin. The trigger record it depends on is produced by the A13 client link surface in M7.

For team shape: this module holds two workstreams with no shared component beyond the underlying event data — a billing engine covering two distinct invoice paths, and a reporting layer. They can be staffed and sequenced independently.

OPEN — Guarantee and replacement period. Invoicing at day 90 removes the need to reverse a pre-confirmation failure. What remains is a failure after day 90 inside a longer guarantee window. If the guarantee period is aligned to the 90-day trial, D25 debit and credit notes stay in the later release; if it is longer, that capability returns to Release 1. Apollo will confirm the period before estimation.
OPEN — Collections and the finance system. The chain ends at invoice raised and receivable recorded. Payment receipt, reconciliation including part-payments and TDS, ageing, reminders and dunning, and credit and debit notes are all outside Release 1. The finance system invoices post to has not been named; Apollo will confirm. Regardless of the answer, build the invoice object addressable by API or file export so the target system attaches later without reopening the module.

Hard dependency: D28 stage-level loss by recruiter cannot be produced without the D14 owner field in M3.

Client letter, 19 August 2026

Apollo Knowledge Services, letter of 19 August 2026, responding to the Statement of Work dated 13 August

Points that apply across the plan rather than to one milestone. The per-milestone points are reproduced in each milestone on the Milestone detail tab.

What complete means at Month 6 — Permanent placement and contractual staffing as live lines of business. Nurses, doctors and allied health professionals as three category modules. Hospital and home healthcare settings as placement destinations. A platform able to receive learner and certification records from the Apollo LMS.
Category is a dimension, not a release — The decision that matters is not when the other categories arrive. It is whether the core is built category-aware, and that is settled in M0 and M1 - in the data model, in the settings layer and in the adapter interfaces - not in R2. A core built to one category makes doctors a migration rather than a module.
Item 15 — Each category module delivered as a working end-to-end path before any part of it is thickened - one client, one requisition, one candidate: sourced, ranked, shortlisted to the client, placed, tracked to day 90 and invoiced. Nurses first.
Item 16 — Acceptance and milestone sign-off tied to gates that produce something a stakeholder can operate, rather than to module completion.
Item 17 — A gate every three to four weeks, with a working demonstration and named Apollo attendees: Shallu Kakkar for fulfilment and the recruiter workflow, Suman for the client-facing surfaces, and Mr. Abednego Peter for clinical quality and the candidate record. A written record at each gate of what was raised and what changed as a result.
Item 18 — The scenario catalogue against which acceptance is measured to be authored jointly and finalised gate by gate, rather than fixed during M1 design. Where feedback at a gate concerns capability already inside the agreed scope, it completes the specification for that capability rather than constituting a change.
Priority — If the delivery period cannot hold both the three category modules and the full R2 remainder, we would rather have three category modules on a thinner R2 than one category module on a complete R2. The category dimension is what becomes expensive to retrofit, because it reaches into the M1 adapters, the M6 weight model and the M8 question sets. The R2 remainder does not.
Drafting point 1 — §5.1 defines the functional boundary as the scope in Sections 2.1 and 2.2. This SOW has no Sections 2.1 or 2.2 - Section 2 is the build sequence and is not subdivided. Please point the clause at the scope register.
Drafting point 2 — §5.1 also states that no external client-portal users are onboarded in the first six months, and separately that portal users for hospitals and home healthcare providers are onboarded in R2, which falls inside the six months. Please reconcile.
Not asking for — No reduction in scope, no change to the six-month delivery period, no reduction in M0, and no change to your dependency logic where it reflects a real constraint.
Milestones M10 to M15 have no module section. Part A records only that five deliverables defer to a later release. Everything this plan says about those six milestones is Hiresense analysis built from the capability names, not from client text.

M0 · F01–F06 · Months 1–2
Platform Foundations
Six things every other milestone needs from the first day: how people sign in, who they are, what they are allowed to do, a record of what they did, one place to hold the configurable lists and waiting periods, and somewhere for uploaded files to live. The client's deliverable list asks for role-based access but never says how anyone logs in, and asks for a requisition owner from the Apollo user list but never creates that list. The remaining foundation work - message sending, the shared list component, environments, the design system, data protection and event recording - is not built here. Each sits in the milestone that first needs it, so nothing is built months before anything uses it.

No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

Everything here is built once and read by every milestone. The audit service is written to by five of them. Roles and permissions govern every screen in the platform.

Records this milestone owns

  • User, Role, Permission - who can sign in and what they can reach. Every milestone reads these.
  • AuditEntry - who did what, when. Append-only, written to by M3, M6, M7, M8 and M9.
  • Setting, ListEntry - the configurable lists and waiting periods, with per-client overrides.
  • Document - every uploaded file, with its versions and retention rule.

How records move

  • User: invited → active → deactivated. A deactivated user cannot sign in but stays visible on their past records.
  • Document: uploaded → scanned → available. Or quarantined if the scan fails.

Not in this milestone

  • Candidate logins. Those are phone-and-code and are built in M4.
  • Client logins. Those come with the client portal in M10.
  • The design system, the shared list component, environments, data protection and event recording. All still required, but each is built in the milestone that first needs it rather than up front.

Needs to exist first

  • Nothing. This milestone goes first.

What breaks without it

  • Every other milestone. Without permissions no screen can restrict anything, and without the audit record the day-90 billing trigger cannot be defended.

M0-I1Sign inF01ScreenXL · 5.0pdAI HighMediumCOMPMedium
Who uses it: All Apollo internal users. How an Apollo user gets into the platform and stays in. D11 asks for role-based access but never says how anyone signs in.

Screens to build

  1. Sign in
    • Takes an email address and a password
    • Signs the user in and opens the console
  2. Forgot password
    • Takes an email address
    • Sends a reset link to that address
  3. Set new password
    • Opened from the reset link
    • Takes a new password twice and saves it

Conditions it must satisfy

  • A user signs in and reaches the console with the navigation their role permits
  • A session times out after the configured idle period and the user is returned to login
  • A password reset link works once and expires
  • Every login, failed login and lockout is written to the audit trail (M0-I4)
  • Candidate logins are completely separate from this - they use phone and code in M4

No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

Two things here are built once and used everywhere. The console shell (navigation, page frame, permissions) is used by every Ops screen in the platform. The registry adapter interface is used by M4 for every identity and credential check. It also carries two pieces of foundation work that nothing before it needs: the design system and the deployment pipeline and environments.

Records this milestone owns

  • User, Role, Permission — who can log in and what they can do. Every other module reads these.
  • MarketAdapter, AdapterCall — the configuration for each outside registry, and a log of every call made to one.
  • MigrationBatch, MigrationRecord, MigrationException — the old-data import: what came in, what loaded, what failed.
  • SystemConfig — platform-wide default settings. M2 falls back to these when a client has none set.

How records move

  • MigrationBatch: staged → profiled → transformed → loaded → reconciled → cut over
  • MigrationRecord: extracted → mapped → loaded. Or extracted → exception → resolved → loaded. Or rejected, with a reason.
  • AdapterCall: requested → responded, giving green, amber or red. Or failed → retried. Or falls back to a person checking it by hand.

Not in this milestone

  • Adapters for the US, Germany and Ireland. India only for now. The interface must be able to take them later without changing the core app.
  • The LMS. Apollo's own team is building it. It will connect by API later. Nothing here depends on it.
  • Any client login. The client portal (D12) is a later milestone, so logins here are for Apollo staff only.
  • Statutory filing (PF, ESIC returns). Not in this contract at all.

Needs to exist first

  • Nothing. This milestone goes first.

What breaks without it

  • Everything. No environment and no login means nothing else can be built or tested. No adapter layer means M4 cannot verify anyone. No migration means the candidate pool is nearly empty when matching starts.

M2 · D1, D13, F08 · Months 2–3
Client, Contract & Rate Card
This holds the client side of the business: the hospital or home-healthcare company, its sites and departments, the rates agreed with it, and the signed contract. Two things here matter more than they look. First, the list of email addresses and WhatsApp numbers belonging to each client — M3 uses it to work out who sent an incoming requisition. Second, the contract terms, because the billing engine in M9 reads them at the moment it decides to raise an invoice.

Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M2 — Client, Contract & Rate Card. Reproduced without alteration.

Holds the client entity with its locations and departments, rate cards by role, skill and location, and the contract record with versioning, renewal alerts and history. It supplies the contract terms the billing triggers read — the day-90 retention terms for permanent, and the timesheet approval terms for contractual — including the per-client windows after which retention is deemed confirmed and a timesheet deemed approved where a client does not respond.

Deliverables: D1, D13

Surfaces: Ops/Admin.

OPEN — D1 user provisioning scope. D1 includes user provisioning with role-based access. D12 (client portal) is deferred and A13 operates without login or account provisioning, so there is no client-side login in Release 1. Confirm that D1 provisioning in Release 1 covers Apollo internal users only.
CLOSED — Deemed-confirmation and deemed-approval windows. Both are configurable per client and held in D13: the day-90 retention deemed-confirmation window, and the timesheet deemed-approval window added with client timesheet approval. Estimate the configurability, not a fixed period. Both require a platform-level default so that a client onboarded without a configured window does not stall invoicing. Apollo has not yet supplied the default values.

No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

The sender list built here is what M3 checks every incoming message against. The client contact record is the most likely place to hold the people who receive the tokenised links in M7. It also carries the shared list component every Ops screen reuses, first needed here for the client and rate card lists.

Records this milestone owns

  • Client, Location, Department — created here. Every other module reads them and none of them writes back.
  • ClientContact — the people at the client. Probably also where link recipients live (see open item OI-A-12).
  • SenderIdentity — an email address, WhatsApp number or HRMS account, mapped to one client. M3 looks up every inbound message here.
  • RateCard, RateCardLine — what we charge, by role, skill and location, with the dates each rate applies from.
  • Contract, ContractVersion — the signed agreement and its terms. M9 reads this when it raises an invoice.
  • ClientWindowConfig — three waiting periods per client: how long before we treat retention as confirmed, how long before we treat a timesheet as approved, and how long before we chase a missing requisition field.

How records move

  • Client: draft → active → suspended → closed
  • Contract: draft → active → renewed (creates a new version) → expired. Or terminated, with a reason.
  • RateCardVersion: draft → in effect → superseded. Old versions are never deleted, because an old requisition must still price against the rate that applied when it was raised.

Not in this milestone

  • Client login and self-service. That is the client portal, milestone M10.
  • Writing contracts from a template. In this milestone contracts are recorded, not drafted.
  • Credit limits and credit control. Billing stops at invoice raised.

Needs to exist first

  • M1 — logins, roles and a running environment.

What breaks without it

  • M3 cannot create a requisition without a client, and cannot identify an inbound sender without the sender list. M7 needs the contract to map an offer to. M9 cannot raise a permanent invoice at all without the contract terms — there is no fallback.

M3 · D2, A1, D14 (thin), F07, F14 · Months 3–4
Demand Intake & Allocation
Clients tell Apollo about vacancies in whatever way suits them — an email, a spreadsheet, a WhatsApp message, a WhatsApp voice note, a feed from their own HR system, or just by telling a recruiter who sits in their hospital. This module takes all of those and turns them into one standard requisition record. It checks whether the required fields are there, chases the client on the same channel they used if anything is missing, and then tracks the vacancy through its life: open, being worked on, paused, changed, partly filled, filled, closed. It also records who owns the vacancy and which recruiters were put on it during the daily morning call.

Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, section A1 Deliverable Inventory. Reproduced without alteration.

As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M3 — Demand Intake & Allocation. Reproduced without alteration.

Captures a requisition from any channel the client already uses and normalises it to a single structured requisition object carrying candidate category, with source retained, completeness scored against required fields and follow-up routed back through the originating channel; it also holds shift templates, live fulfilment status and aging, and the demand lifecycle of hold, revise and close. It attaches an owner to the requisition and records the recruiter allocation that the daily morning call already makes.

Deliverables: D2, A1, D14 (thin slice)

Surfaces: Ops/Admin. Email, client-format spreadsheets, WhatsApp text and voice notes and client HRMS/ATS are ingestion paths rather than rendered surfaces; verbal capture by a site-resident recruiter is entered through Ops/Admin.

OPEN — A1 channel count in Release 1. A1 names six ingestion channels including the client portal. D12 is deferred, so five are available in Release 1. Confirm the portal channel is specified but not built, rather than dropped from A1.
OPEN — A9 requisition-side fields. A9 now carries a per-category attribute set on both the requisition and the candidate record, so requisition granularity differs by category. The requisition-side fields are not explicitly scoped into D2. Ownership of that half of A9 needs assigning between M3 and M4.

Scope boundary: the prioritisation engine and the requisition approval workflow remain in the later release. What sits in Release 1 is an owner field and capture of an allocation made outside the system — a record, not an assignment engine. The inbound parsing pipeline is built here under A1 and consumed by A14 in M5.

Added by the client, 19 August 2026
Apollo Knowledge Services, letter of 19 August 2026, responding to the Statement of Work dated 13 August. Reproduced without alteration.
Item 6 — Category on the requisition under D2, together with the requisition-side half of the A9 attribute set. Ownership of that half is currently unassigned between M3 and M4.
Item 13c — An inbound path by which a learner becomes a candidate in the supply base.
No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

The message-receiving pipeline built here — receive, queue, work out who sent it, parse it, decide what kind of message it is, handle failures, retry — is built once in this module. M5 reuses the same pipeline for candidate replies. So it must be built to accept a second kind of message from the start, not just requisitions. It also carries two pieces of foundation work first needed here: the message-sending plumbing the follow-up chase runs on, and the event recording the M9 reports read later.

Records this milestone owns

  • InboundRecord — one row per incoming message or file. Keeps the channel, the sender, the time, and the original raw content. Kept forever, even after the requisition is created, so a disputed vacancy can be traced back to what the client actually sent.
  • Requisition — the main record. Client, site, role, how many positions, candidate category, the category-specific fields, the rate to use, how complete it is, which fields are missing, who owns it, its status and its age.
  • RequisitionVersion — written every time the requisition is changed, recording what changed, when, and who did it.
  • ShiftDemandLine — contract staffing only. One row per shift, generated from a shift pattern.
  • AllocationRecord — which recruiters were put on this vacancy, when, and by whom. Rows are only ever added, never changed.
  • StatusTransition — every status change with a timestamp. M9 cannot report where candidates drop out without this.

How records move

  • InboundRecord: received → identified (we know the client) or unmatched (we do not). Then parsed, or unparseable. Then consumed once a requisition is made from it.
  • Requisition: incomplete or complete → awaiting client (we are chasing missing fields) → complete → open → in fulfilment → partially filled → filled
  • Requisition: in fulfilment ↔ on hold. Putting it on hold stops the age counter; releasing it restarts from where it stopped, not from zero.
  • Requisition: any state → closed. A reason from the configured list is required.

Not in this milestone

  • Working out which vacancies matter most, and any approval step before a vacancy goes live. That is milestone M14.
  • The system suggesting or balancing which recruiter gets which vacancy. Here we only record the decision made on the morning call.
  • Enforcing a cap on how many vacancies a recruiter holds. The 15-16 norm is not enforced.
  • The client portal as a way of sending vacancies in. Listed as a channel so it can be switched on later, but not built here.
  • Automatically merging duplicate requisitions. We show a suspected duplicate and let a person decide.

Needs to exist first

  • M1 for logins and environment. M2 for the client, sites and rate card. M4 for the definition of the category-specific fields.

What breaks without it

  • M6 ranks against the requisition — no requisition, no ranking, and both business flows stop at step one. M7 offers and client links quote the requisition. M8 counts placements against it. M9 needs the owner and allocation to report by recruiter. M5 reuses the message pipeline built here.

  • Language coverage is proven against field-recorded audio, not studio samples

M4 · D3, D4, D16, A2, A9, F13 · Months 2–4
Candidate Record & Credentials
This holds everything about the healthcare professionals: nurses, doctors and allied health staff. There are two separate pools — one for permanent placements and one for shift work — but both share the same set of credential records. The module covers checking who someone is and that their registration is real (using the adapter layer from M1), a work queue for the verification team, and the candidate mobile app. The app is where candidates sign up with their phone number, upload a CV, find jobs and shifts, apply, and check in at the start of a shift.

As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M4 — Candidate Record & Credentials. Reproduced without alteration.

Holds the candidate as a record spanning two supply bases against a shared credential object carrying issuer, validity period and verification method, with document OCR, identity verification behind the M1 adapter layer, and a verification queue with pendency tracking. It covers three candidate categories — nurses, doctors and allied health — with A9 held as a per-category attribute set, and includes the candidate mobile app: self-registration with phone and OTP, resume upload and parsing, duplicate detection and merge, job and shift discovery, application and shift tracking, and shift check-in.

Deliverables: D3, D4, D16, A2, A9

Surfaces: Ops/Admin (candidate record, verification queue, pendency tracker) and Candidate App.

OPEN — Clinical and communication rating field. D17 is descoped, but the candidate record retains a clinical and communication rating attribute. No producing system is specified, and whether D18 ranking weights that attribute is not stated. Until the production method is decided, the field is present and unpopulated.
CLOSED — Candidate categories. Resolved. Release 1 covers nurses, doctors and allied health. Category is a field on the requisition and the candidate record; A9 becomes a per-category attribute set — specialty and acuity for nurses, specialty, case-mix and grade for doctors, discipline, equipment and technique competency for allied health. The D15 taxonomy is not revived as a deliverable; it is absorbed into A9. The credential model in D16 is unchanged, so additional registries resolve as adapters and configuration.

D3 is a three-way shared build and a hard dependency. D3 sits in M4, but shift discovery, acceptance and check-in serve M8, and its response surface is one of the two A14 channels in M5. Because shift attendance is captured by app check-in with no alternative route, D3 is a hard dependency of the contractual invoice chain — no app, no attendance, no shift invoice. Sequence it against all three consumers.

Added by the client, 19 August 2026
Apollo Knowledge Services, letter of 19 August 2026, responding to the Statement of Work dated 13 August. Reproduced without alteration.
Item 2 — Per-category attribute sets under A9: specialty and acuity for nurses; specialty, case-mix and grade for doctors; discipline, equipment and technique competency for allied health.
Item 13a — Resolution of a learner identity against a candidate record, since the two register through different funnels.
Item 13b — A certification and course-completion attribute on the candidate record that D18 can read.
No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

The mobile app is used by three modules. It sits here, but shift discovery and shift check-in are how M8 gets its attendance data, and the app is one of two ways candidates answer availability questions in M5. Because shift check-in is the only way attendance reaches the system, no app means no attendance, which means no timesheet and no shift invoice. Plan the app against all three, not just this module. It also carries the data protection work - consent, residency and retention - which is first needed the moment candidate personal data is captured.

Records this milestone owns

  • Candidate — the person. Holds their category, which pool they are in, and their availability confidence score (M5 writes it, M6 reads it).
  • Credential — a registration or qualification: who issued it, how long it is valid, and how it was checked. Shared across both pools.
  • CandidateDocument — CVs, ID scans, certificates. The text pulled out by OCR is attached to these.
  • VerificationCase — one check, with a green, amber or red result, and a note of how long it has been waiting.
  • CategoryAttributeSet — the extra fields that differ by category: specialty and acuity for nurses; specialty, case-mix and grade for doctors; discipline, equipment and technique for allied health.
  • Application, ShiftAcceptance — what the candidate has applied for or agreed to work.
  • DuplicateCandidate — two records that look like the same person, waiting for someone to decide.

How records move

  • Candidate: registered → profile complete → verification pending → verified, partly verified, or failed
  • Credential: claimed → submitted → being checked → verified (green), flagged (amber) or rejected (red) → expired
  • VerificationCase: queued → in progress → resolved, waiting on an outside registry, or handed to a person to check manually
  • Application: applied → shortlisted → interviewing → offered → joined. Or rejected, or withdrawn.

Not in this milestone

  • Bulk candidate loads from campuses or partners, walk-in registration and referral tagging. Removed from this contract; Apollo handles these by hand.
  • Face recognition, biometrics and cheating detection in assessments. Later release.
  • Blocking a shift check-in based on where the phone is. We record the location but do not act on it. Enforcing it is milestone M12.
  • Statutory joining forms such as PF and gratuity nomination. That is milestone M11.
  • Producing the clinical and communication rating. The field exists on the record but nothing fills it in — see open item OI-A-07.

Needs to exist first

  • M1 for the adapter layer — without it no identity or credential check can run. M1 also supplies the migrated candidate pool.

What breaks without it

  • M5 writes availability onto the candidate record. M6 ranks on credentials, category fields and availability, and cannot rank if any of the three is missing. M8 gets shift attendance from the app. M7 quotes the candidate on shortlists and offers.

M5 · D10, D29, A3, A4, A5, A14 · Months 3–5
Engagement & Availability
Apollo needs to know which nurses are actually available right now. This module decides who to contact, how often, and on which channel, then sends the message over WhatsApp, SMS, email, app push or an automated phone call. When the person answers, it records the answer in one standard format and resets a freshness score on their record. If nobody hears from them, that score decays over time, and M6 ranks them lower as a result. The shift pool gets contacted every three to four days; the permanent pool less often.
Release 116 items89 conditions107.5pd12 high riskOps/Admin, Candidate App, WhatsApp, Voice

As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M5 — Engagement & Availability. Reproduced without alteration.

Determines which candidates to contact, on what schedule, through which channel and with which question set, configurable per segment, and delivers on that decision over WhatsApp, SMS, email, app push and outbound voice with template management, trigger configuration, delivery status, retry, opt-out, preference management and a per-user notification log. It captures the response on a single structured schema across the WhatsApp interactive template and the candidate app, writes it back to the candidate record, and resets the availability confidence value that otherwise decays on a configurable schedule per base.

Deliverables: D10, D29, A3, A4, A5, A14

Surfaces: Ops/Admin (cadence configuration, template management, notification and response logs), Candidate App, WhatsApp interactive templates, outbound voice.

A3, A4, A5 and A14 form a closed loop: the cadence engine selects on confidence, delivery goes out, the response returns on a structured schema, and the confirmed response resets confidence, which determines the next selection. None of the four is independently operable. Treat them as one unit for estimation and sequencing — see A5.

Base B cadence runs across the whole base every three to four days, and Base A on a longer cycle; the cadence is configurable per segment, so per-channel message volumes are a function of configuration rather than a fixed number.

CLOSED — Ownership of the shared inbound pipeline. Assigned. The channel receiver, queue, sender identity resolution, parser dispatch, intent classification, unparseable-message handling and retry are built once under A1 in M3. A14 consumes that stack with a candidate-response intent schema. HireSense returns three estimate lines: the pipeline once, A1 schema and routing, A14 schema and routing.
OPEN — Free-text fallback. A14 states that free-text responses fall back to parsing. No accuracy threshold, confidence handling or human-review path is specified for parsed responses that then reset availability confidence in A3.
OPEN — A5 provider. Apollo will confirm the outbound voice provider. The items to be estimated are the integration and the response extraction schema, not the voice service itself.
Added by the client, 19 August 2026
Apollo Knowledge Services, letter of 19 August 2026, responding to the Statement of Work dated 13 August. Reproduced without alteration.
Item 7 — Six supply segments rather than two. A2 gives Base A and Base B; across three categories that is six, each with its own acquisition motion, decay schedule, contact cadence and ranking weights.
No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

Four pieces here only work as a set: the engine that picks who to contact, the sending layer, the reply capture, and the freshness score. Build any three of them and none of them does anything useful. Estimate and schedule them together, not separately.

Records this milestone owns

  • NotificationTemplate — the message text per channel. WhatsApp templates also need approval from the provider before they can be sent, which takes time.
  • NotificationLog — one row per message sent, per person, with whether it arrived.
  • ContactPreference, OptOut — which channels a candidate is willing to be contacted on.
  • Segment, CadenceRule — a group of candidates and how often to contact them.
  • AvailabilityResponse — the reply, in one format whether it came from WhatsApp, the app or a phone call.
  • VoiceCall, VoiceTranscript — the automated call log and what was said.
  • AvailabilityConfidence — a freshness score. It lives on the candidate record in M4, is written here, and is read by M6.

How records move

  • Notification: queued → sent → delivered. Or failed → retried → given up on.
  • AvailabilityConfidence: confirmed (reset to full) → decaying on a schedule → stale → picked up again by the contact engine
  • AvailabilityResponse: received → understood clearly, or unclear and sent to a person to check → confirmed
  • VoiceCall: queued → dialling → connected → finished with an answer. Or no answer → tried again later.

Not in this milestone

  • Handling replies to SMS and email. We send on those channels but only WhatsApp and the app can receive a structured reply — see open item OI-A-17.
  • Step-by-step guided conversations over WhatsApp. WhatsApp sends messages and fixed-button templates; anything guided happens in the app.
  • Reporting on campaign performance beyond the send log. That reporting sits in M9.
  • The phone-call service itself. We build the integration and the answer extraction; Apollo buys the service — and has not yet said from whom.

Needs to exist first

  • M4 for the candidate records and the fields availability is written onto. M3 for the message pipeline the reply capture reuses.

What breaks without it

  • M6 ranks on the freshness score. M8 sends trial check-ins through this layer. M7 sends offers and client links through it. If reply capture fails, nothing ever resets and the whole pool goes stale.

Items — 16 to build · design 9.5pd, build 66.5pd, QA 31.5pd

  • Per-channel message volume is a function of configuration, which is why volume must be modelled (OI-HS-05)

Who uses it: System. The freshness score: it drops over time and resets when someone confirms they are available.

Screens to build

  1. No screen
    • Holds a freshness score on each candidate that falls over time
    • Falls on a different schedule for each candidate category and pool - six schedules, not two
    • Resets to full when the candidate confirms they are available
    • Serves that score and the date last confirmed to M6

Conditions it must satisfy

  • Confidence decays on the configured schedule per base and resets on a confirmed response (Part A M5)
  • Base A availability is expressed as a notice period; Base B as time windows within a travel radius

  • M6 can filter and weight on both the value and the recency (Part A stage 7)
  • A response parsed from free text below the confidence threshold does not silently reset confidence (OI-A-08)
  • Field schema ownership between M4 and M5 is fixed before either writes

M6 · D5, D18, A8 · Months 4–5
Matching & Ranking
Given a vacancy, produce a ranked list of candidates, best first, with a plain explanation of why each one ranked where they did and which requirements they do not meet. The weightings are different for each candidate category and each pool, so there are at least six sets. Recruiters can search manually and can move someone up or down the list, and every override is recorded. When a client or interviewer rejects someone with a reason, that reason feeds back in and affects later rankings.

Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M6 — Matching & Ranking. Reproduced without alteration.

Produces a ranked candidate set against a requisition, exposing the rationale for each rank together with unmet or partially met criteria, applying different weightings per candidate category and supply base, with saved searches, talent segments, advanced search on skill, location, experience and status, and recruiter override. It consumes selection outcomes and structured rejection reasons written back from M7 so that subsequent ranking reflects them.

Surfaces: Ops/Admin.

OPEN — Manual shortlist assembly. D5 is described as retaining search and submission while manual shortlist building is superseded by ranked output. Whether hand-assembly survives as a recruiter fallback, or whether recruiter override on the ranked set is the only manual path, is not stated.
OPEN — Ranking input weighting. D18 consumes the credential record (D16), specialty and acuity (A9), availability confidence and recency (A3), and written-back outcomes (A8). With three candidate categories and two supply bases, the weight sets multiply accordingly, and the inputs themselves differ by category. The relative weighting model across those inputs, and its configurability, is not specified.

Item 4 — Per-category ranking weight profiles under D18, configurable rather than coded. Both the weights and the inputs differ by category.
Context — Your note records that the D18 weighting model is not specified. Under the current sequence M6 completes around month three or four, and that is the first time a recruiter sees a ranked shortlist. A crude ranked list on three fields, put in front of recruiters against a live requisition in week eight, gives you the weighting from their overrides instead of from a specification.
No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

The list of rejection reasons in M7 and the data the ranking model reads are one design, not two. If the ranking model has no slot for outcome data, feeding rejections back in later means rebuilding it. Agree both before either module is built.

Records this milestone owns

  • RankedSet — one ranked list for one vacancy or one shift, recording which weighting set produced it and when.
  • RankEntry — one candidate in that list: their score, why they scored it, and what they do not meet.
  • WeightSet — how much each factor counts. One per candidate category per pool, so at least six.
  • RecruiterOverride — a manual move up or down, with a reason. Rows are only added, never changed.
  • SavedSearch, TalentSegment — searches and candidate groups recruiters reuse.
  • OutcomeSignal — rejection reasons and selection results fed back from M7.

How records move

  • RankedSet: generating → current → out of date (once the vacancy changes or new outcome data arrives)
  • RankEntry: ranked → shortlisted → sent to client → outcome recorded
  • OutcomeSignal: captured → written back → included in the next ranking run. This happens in the background and does not hold anything up.

Not in this milestone

  • Retraining the model automatically. We apply a configured set of weights; continuous learning is not in this contract.
  • Showing candidates their own match score. Ranking is an internal tool only.
  • Sending a shortlist to a client without a recruiter pressing the button.
  • Using the clinical and communication rating. Nothing produces that value — see open item OI-A-07.

Needs to exist first

  • M3 for the vacancy. M4 for credentials and category fields. M5 for the freshness score. All three are needed; ranking cannot run on a partial set.

What breaks without it

  • M7 takes the shortlist. Everything after M7 depends on this indirectly.
