M6-I3Ranked candidatesD18ScreenXL · 6.0pdAI MediumHighMODELLow
Who uses it: Recruiter. The ranked list of candidates for a vacancy, best first.

Screens to build

  1. Ranked candidates
    • Lists candidates for a requisition, best first, with their score
    • Shows each candidate's credentials, specialty, availability and location
    • Marks the set as out of date when the requisition changes
    • Runs the same way against a single shift for shift work
  2. Why this rank
    • Opens from a candidate in the ranked list
    • Lists which requirements the candidate meets, partly meets and does not meet
    • Shows how fresh the availability behind the rank is
  3. Override rank
    • Moves a candidate up or down the list
    • Requires a reason for the move

Conditions it must satisfy

  • A ranked set is produced against a requisition with rationale and unmet or partially met criteria exposed per rank (Part A stage 8)
  • Weights differ by candidate category and supply base - six combinations minimum (Part A M6)
  • A set generated before a requisition revision is marked stale and regenerable
  • Contractual ranking runs against a shift demand line, not only a requisition (Part A contractual stage 6)
  • Ranking cannot run where category, credentials or availability are missing - it fails visibly rather than ranking on a partial input set
  • The weighting model across inputs is unspecified (OI-A-11)
  • Rationale names the specific criteria met, partially met and unmet, not a generic score breakdown (Part A stage 8)
  • A candidate ranked highly on stale availability shows that staleness in the rationale
  • The rationale is defensible to a client without exposing the weight model itself
  • Verification state is visible in the rationale so a shortlist can be defended on it (Part A dependency table)
  • Where the clinical and communication rating is unpopulated, its absence is stated rather than silently weighted at zero (OI-A-07)
  • Overrides are recorded where applied and visible in the submitted shortlist (Part A stage 8)
  • An override requires a reason, so override patterns are analysable
  • Overriding does not silently retrain the model - Release 1 applies a configured weight set
  • Override history is retained per requisition and exposed to M9 reporting

M7 · D6, D20, A13 · Months 4–5
Selection, Offer & Client Response
This covers interviews, the decision, and the offer. Propose interview slots, handle changes and cancellations, record feedback, then accept, hold or reject with a reason picked from a list. If accepted, generate the offer from a template, send it over WhatsApp, track it, chase it, and get it signed. It also builds the way clients interact with the platform: a one-off web link sent to them over the channel they already use. There is no client login anywhere in this milestone. The same link mechanism is used four ways — responding to a shortlist, three trial check-ins, and approving a timesheet — but only the 90-day one causes an invoice to be raised.

Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M7 — Selection, Offer & Client Response. Reproduced without alteration.

Runs slot proposal and confirmation, reschedule and cancellation, feedback capture, the accept / hold / reject decision with mandatory structured rejection reasons against a configurable taxonomy and a selection audit trail, then generates, delivers, tracks and expires the offer through to digital signature and offer-to-contract mapping. It also provides the tokenised client link mechanism, built once and instantiated by configurable link type — shortlist response, trial check-in at day 30, 60 and 90, and timesheet approval — reached without login or account provisioning and delivered through the channel the client already uses.

Deliverables: D6, D20, A13

Only the day-90 retention instance is a billing trigger — day 30 and day 60 capture signal and must not create a second trigger path. Price the mechanism once and each type as an instance.

No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

Build the link mechanism once, then configure four types on top of it. Three other modules use those links, so build it before them. Be careful that the day 30 and day 60 links never gain the power to trigger billing — only day 90 does.

Records this milestone owns

  • Interview — slot, format, panel and state, plus the history of any changes.
  • InterviewFeedback — structured feedback from each interviewer.
  • SelectionDecision — accept, hold or reject, with a reason from the configured list.
  • RejectionReason — the configured list. Shared with the ranking feedback in M6.
  • Offer, OfferVersion — the document, picked by state, role and skill, with an expiry date and reminders.
  • ClientLink — a one-off web address: its type, who it went to, when it was sent, opened and answered.
  • LinkRecipient — the person at the client who received it, recorded so the answer can be attributed. How these are held is not yet decided — see open item OI-A-12.

How records move

  • Interview: proposed → confirmed → held → feedback recorded. Or rescheduled, or cancelled with a reason.
  • SelectionDecision: pending → accepted, held, or rejected with a reason
  • Offer: draft → generated → sent → opened → signed. Or expired, or declined. Then linked to the contract.
  • ClientLink: issued → sent → opened → answered. Or expired → treated as agreed (only for the day-90 and timesheet types, and only after the waiting period in the contract).

Not in this milestone

  • Any client login or client account. Links need no password. The portal is milestone M10.
  • AI video interviews. Later phase.
  • Connecting to interviewers' external calendars. Slots are proposed and confirmed inside the platform.
  • Running background checks at offer stage. Verification happens earlier, in M4.
  • Letting the day 30 or day 60 link trigger an invoice. Those capture information only.

Needs to exist first

  • M6 for the shortlist. M2 for the contract to map the offer to and the waiting periods. M5 to send offers and links.

What breaks without it

  • M8 needs the accepted offer to create a joining event — without offers the whole permanent flow stops after selection. M9 needs the day-90 link answer as its billing trigger. M6 gets rejection reasons back. If the link mechanism is late, the client side of M7 and both invoice paths in M9 stop, because there is no other way to reach a client.

M8 · D7, A6, D22 (thin) · Months 5–6
Placement, Trial & Shift Attendance
Once someone accepts an offer, this module tracks them joining and then checks in on them at day 0, 7, 30, 60 and 90. The candidate is asked whether they are being paid on time, whether they have a preceptor, and whether their accommodation is sorted. The client is asked at day 30 and 60 how it is going, and at day 90 whether the person is still there — that last answer is what lets Apollo invoice. Separately, for shift workers, this module collects attendance from the app check-ins and builds it into a timesheet, which the client then approves through a link.

As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M8 — Placement, Trial & Shift Attendance. Reproduced without alteration.

Records joining confirmation with date tracking and drop-out capture with reasons, then tracks a configurable set of check-in events across day 0, 7, 30, 60 and 90 capturing attendance, salary payment timeliness, preceptor assignment and accommodation status, with client intent captured at day 60 and an auditable client-acknowledged record at day 90. It also carries shift-level attendance captured by check-in on the candidate app, and the timesheet, which now holds an approval state set by the client through an A13 link; leave categorisation, overtime treatment and the internal approval routing workflow remain in the later release.

Deliverables: D7, A6, D22 (thin slice)

Surfaces: Ops/Admin (trial dashboard, attendance and timesheet), Candidate App (shift check-in and candidate-side responses), outbound voice/WhatsApp for candidate-side check-ins, Client link surfaces for the day 30, 60 and 90 records and for timesheet approval.

CLOSED — Shift attendance capture mechanism. Attendance is captured by check-in on the candidate app, replacing the Excel import in the original D22. Release 1 records a location stamp against each check-in without enforcing a boundary; geo-fencing moves off the exclusion list to a named Phase 2 item so the field exists when enforcement is specified. Pilot runs on in-house nurses.
CLOSED — Client timesheet approval. Added. The timesheet carries an approval state set by the client through an A13 link type — the same mechanism as the day-90 confirmation, second instance. D25 invoices against an approved timesheet rather than raw attendance. A deemed-approval window is configurable per client in D13, with a platform-level default so an unconfigured client does not stall invoicing.
Added by the client, 19 August 2026
Apollo Knowledge Services, letter of 19 August 2026, responding to the Statement of Work dated 13 August. Reproduced without alteration.
Item 5 — Per-category trial question sets under A6. Your own scope notes records that these differ by category.
Item 8 — Specialty privileging. A doctor placed at a hospital ordinarily requires privileges granted through that hospital's credentialing process, over and above council registration. There is no equivalent step in the nurse chain and nothing in the SOW covers it. We need to know whether it is in or out.
Item 11 — A homecare trial question set, and an attendance verification method appropriate to it. A6 captures preceptor assignment and accommodation, neither of which exists in a home. D22 records a location stamp without enforcing it, and geo-fencing enforcement only arrives in D23. In homecare, location is the only verification available, so unenforced is materially weaker there than in a facility.
Item 12 — An export or file handoff from the approved timesheet to a manual payroll process. Payroll sits at step ten of ten; until it is live, staff are paid by hand and the approved timesheet is the only clean handoff point.
No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

The check-in questions differ by candidate category, so they use the same category setup as M4. The client-facing parts of the trial run on the link mechanism built in M7.

Records this milestone owns

  • Placement — the person in the job: joining date, which vacancy, which contract. The 90-day clock hangs off this.
  • DropOut — someone who did not join or left early, with a reason. The position goes back to M3 as open.
  • TrialEvent — the day 0, 7, 30, 60 and 90 check-ins, on both the candidate and client side.
  • TrialResponse — the answers: attendance, whether pay arrived on time, preceptor, accommodation.
  • RetentionRecord — the day-90 answer, or the automatic version if the client never replied. This is the evidence behind the invoice.
  • ShiftAttendance — check-in and check-out, with where the phone was at the time (recorded, not enforced).
  • Timesheet — a period of shifts, with a state showing whether the client has approved it.

How records move

  • Placement: joined → in trial → trial survived, or trial ended with a reason
  • Placement: offered → dropped out with a reason (the position goes back to open in M3)
  • TrialEvent: scheduled → due → answered, escalated to a phone call, or missed
  • ShiftAttendance: expected → checked in → checked out. Or no show, or a problem raised → resolved.
  • Timesheet: building → built (not yet approved) → sent for approval → approved, or automatically treated as approved after the waiting period → invoiced

Not in this milestone

  • Paying the nurses. Payroll runs outside the platform for this whole period — that is milestone M13.
  • Requests and approvals for salary changes, advances and holds. Milestone M12.
  • Joining paperwork and statutory forms. Milestone M11.
  • Leave types, overtime rules and internal approval routing on timesheets. Milestone M15.
  • Blocking a check-in based on location. We store the location but do not act on it. Milestone M12.

Needs to exist first

  • M7 for the accepted offer and the link mechanism. M4 for the app, which is the only way attendance arrives.

What breaks without it

  • M9 needs the day-90 record to raise a permanent invoice, and the approved timesheet to raise a shift invoice. M3 needs the fill and drop-out counts.

M8-I7Shift attendanceD22ScreenL · 4.5pdAI HighHighSHAREDMedium
Who uses it: Fulfilment lead, Finance. Every shift, expected and actual, built from app check-ins.

Screens to build

  1. Shift attendance
    • Lists each shift with who was expected, when they checked in and out, and where the phone was
    • Filters by client, date and candidate
    • Marks shifts with no check-in
    • Marks a home healthcare check-in that was not at the visit address
    • Corrects a time by hand, requiring a reason
  2. Resolve a problem
    • Filters to shifts with a missed check-in or check-out
    • Resolves one with a reason before the timesheet is built

Conditions it must satisfy

  • Attendance is recorded at shift level against a check-in and a location stamp (Part A contractual stage 8)
  • The location stamp is recorded and not enforced; geo-fencing is a named Phase 2 item (Part A CLOSED item)
  • A no-show is distinguishable from a missing check-in
  • A manual correction requires a reason and is audited, since it affects an invoice (M0-I4)
  • There is no alternative attendance route - no app means no attendance (Part A coupling 7)
  • An exception is resolved before the timesheet is compiled for that period
  • A missed check-out does not silently become a full shift or a zero shift; it is resolved explicitly
  • Resolution is attributed and audited, since it changes billable hours
  • A systemic cause - an app outage, for instance - can be resolved in bulk
  • Unresolved exceptions are visible on the timesheet before it is sent for approval
  • A check-in away from the expected location is flagged for a person to resolve; whether it is blocked outright is a setting in M12-I5

M9 · D8, A7, D25 (thin), D9, D28, A11 · Months 5–6
Billing, Revenue & Reporting
Two separate jobs sit here. The first is billing: raise a permanent placement invoice once the client confirms at day 90 that the person is still there, and raise shift invoices from approved timesheets. Both must keep the evidence that triggered them, because Apollo may be asked months later to justify a charge. The second is reporting: how many vacancies get filled, how fast, where candidates drop out, how each recruiter and each intake channel performs, and how the rate agreed compares to the standard rate. These two halves share nothing except the underlying event data and can be staffed separately.
Release 117 items78 conditions88.5pd6 high riskOps/Admin

As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, System Overview, Release 1, version 2, section A2 Module Structure — M9 — Billing, Revenue & Reporting. Reproduced without alteration.

Generates the permanent placement invoice on the day-90 client-acknowledged retention record rather than at placement, holding that acknowledgement as an auditable trigger record capable of supporting a billing query, and generates shift-level invoices from the client-approved timesheet and contract for the contractual scope. It reports funnel and conversion, fill rate, time-to-fill, trial survival, stage-level conversion loss by client and by recruiter, channel and recruiter performance, the monthly MIS pack, and rate agreed against baseline by client, role, specialty and acuity.

Deliverables: D8, A7, D25 (thin slice), D9, D28, A11

Surfaces: Ops/Admin. The trigger record it depends on is produced by the A13 client link surface in M7.

For team shape: this module holds two workstreams with no shared component beyond the underlying event data — a billing engine covering two distinct invoice paths, and a reporting layer. They can be staffed and sequenced independently.

OPEN — Guarantee and replacement period. Invoicing at day 90 removes the need to reverse a pre-confirmation failure. What remains is a failure after day 90 inside a longer guarantee window. If the guarantee period is aligned to the 90-day trial, D25 debit and credit notes stay in the later release; if it is longer, that capability returns to Release 1. Apollo will confirm the period before estimation.
OPEN — Collections and the finance system. The chain ends at invoice raised and receivable recorded. Payment receipt, reconciliation including part-payments and TDS, ageing, reminders and dunning, and credit and debit notes are all outside Release 1. The finance system invoices post to has not been named; Apollo will confirm. Regardless of the answer, build the invoice object addressable by API or file export so the target system attaches later without reopening the module.

Hard dependency: D28 stage-level loss by recruiter cannot be produced without the D14 owner field in M3.

Added by the client, 19 August 2026
Apollo Knowledge Services, letter of 19 August 2026, responding to the Statement of Work dated 13 August. Reproduced without alteration.
Item 9 — Billing trigger by line and by category. D8 and A7 fire on a day-90 confirmed retention record, which is built for permanent placement. A locum doctor bills per session against an approved timesheet, which is the D25 path. That permanent and locum use different triggers is not stated anywhere.
No client text for this milestone

M0 is Hiresense-defined. These capabilities are assumed by the client specification but named nowhere in it, so there is nothing to quote. Everything in this milestone is Hiresense analysis.

Built once, used elsewhere

Nothing is shared between the billing engine and the reporting layer except the event data underneath. They can be built by different people at the same time.

Records this milestone owns

  • Invoice, InvoiceLine — two kinds: a permanent placement fee (5-6% of salary), and a shift invoice.
  • BillingTrigger — the evidence that allowed the invoice: either the day-90 confirmation or the approved timesheet.
  • Receivable — money owed against the invoice. This is where the process stops in this contract.
  • ReportingEvent — events collected from every module and reshaped for reporting.
  • RateBaseline, RateVariance — the standard rate against what was actually agreed.

How records move

  • BillingTrigger: pending → confirmed by the client, or automatically treated as confirmed after the waiting period → used by an invoice
  • Invoice: draft → raised → sent to the finance system. A receivable opens.

Not in this milestone

  • Receiving payments, matching them off, part-payments, TDS, chasing overdue money, and credit or debit notes. All milestone M15.
  • The finance system itself. Nobody has said which one it is, so we build a generic export it can attach to later.
  • Forecasting demand. Not in this contract.
  • Any client-facing dashboard. Clients only see the links from M7 until the portal in M10.

Needs to exist first

  • M8 for the day-90 record and the approved timesheet. M7 for the client link that captures the confirmation. M2 for the contract terms read at the moment of invoicing. M3 for the owner field the recruiter reports need. Events from every module.

What breaks without it

  • Nothing inside the platform. The chain ends at invoice raised and money owed recorded.

    • Shows the rejection reasons behind those losses

Conditions it must satisfy

  • Loss by client and by stage is computed from StatusTransition timestamps (M3-I28)
  • Loss by recruiter cannot be produced at all without the D14 owner and allocation (Part A dependency table)
  • Loss reasons come from the structured rejection taxonomy in M7, not free text (M0-I5)
  • The report is correct retrospectively only if events were written at the time (M3-I31)
  • Recruiter attribution uses the allocation history, covering the period the recruiter held the requisition (M3 D14 BR3)

M10 · D12 · Months 5–6
Client Portal
A proper logged-in website for clients. Until now clients have only had one-off links with no password. This gives them accounts: they can raise vacancies themselves, see how their vacancies are progressing, look at shortlists, respond to candidates, approve timesheets and download invoices. Once this exists, the client portal also becomes a sixth way requisitions arrive, which M3 already allows for in its channel list.

As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, section A1 Deliverable Inventory. Reproduced without alteration.

Release 1 comprises thirty-four deliverables: the eleven already scoped to Release 1, nine brought across from Release 2 (six in full, three as thin slices), and fourteen added at revision. Five deliverables defer to a later release and five are descoped from this SOW; those ten are recorded in the scope revision and are not listed here.

The client specification carries no module section for these deliverables. Everything below is Hiresense analysis, not client text.

Built once, used elsewhere

This adds a whole new group of users. The role and permission model from M1 has to stretch to cover people outside Apollo, and every screen needs to be checked so a client only ever sees their own data.

Records this milestone owns

  • ClientUser — a login for someone at the client. Separate from Apollo staff logins.
  • ClientUserRole — what that person can do and which sites they can see.
  • PortalSession — logins and activity, kept for audit.

How records move

  • ClientUser: invited → registered → active → suspended

Not in this milestone

  • Clients editing their own rate card or contract terms. Those stay with Apollo.
  • Clients seeing candidate personal data beyond what the shortlist already shows.

Needs to exist first

  • M1 for the permission model, extended to outside users. M2 for the client and contact records. M3, M6, M7, M8 and M9 for everything the portal displays.

What breaks without it

  • M3 gains a sixth intake channel. The one-off links from M7 stay in place for clients who never sign in.

Items — 8 to build · design 10.5pd, build 22.0pd, QA 11.5pd

    • Takes an email address and a password
    • Signs the client user in and opens the portal
  2. Set up account
    • Opened from an invitation
    • Takes a password and activates the account

Conditions it must satisfy

  • A client user completes set-up from an invite and can sign in afterwards
  • An expired invite can be reissued without creating a second account
  • A client user cannot reach any Apollo internal screen
  • Sessions expire and require a fresh login
  • Client identity is kept entirely separate from Apollo staff identity

The paperwork side of someone joining as a shift worker. Today this is collected and stored outside the platform. Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, section A1 Deliverable Inventory. Reproduced without alteration.

Release 1 comprises thirty-four deliverables: the eleven already scoped to Release 1, nine brought across from Release 2 (six in full, three as thin slices), and fourteen added at revision. Five deliverables defer to a later release and five are descoped from this SOW; those ten are recorded in the scope revision and are not listed here.

Built once, used elsewhere

The document upload and storage built for M4 credentials is reused here. The guided flow lives in the same mobile app.

Records this milestone owns

  • AssociateProfile — the extra employment details needed once someone is actually working, beyond their candidate record.
  • StatutoryForm — PF and UAN transfer, gratuity nomination and the rest, with a completion state each.
  • OnboardingChecklist — which documents are needed and which have arrived.

How records move

  • AssociateProfile: not started → in progress → complete → verified
  • StatutoryForm: pending → submitted → verified. Or returned to the candidate with a reason.

Not in this milestone

  • Filing the statutory returns themselves. Filing is outside this contract entirely.
  • Onboarding for permanent placements. Those join the client directly, not Apollo.

Needs to exist first

  • M4 for the candidate record and document storage. M8 for the placement that triggers onboarding.

What breaks without it

  • M13 payroll needs a complete associate profile and statutory forms before anyone can be paid.

M12 · D23 · Months 5–6
Workforce Operations
The day-to-day changes that happen once people are working: salary revisions for one person or in bulk, salary advances, and putting a salary on hold or releasing it. Each of these needs two people — one to request it and one to approve it — so nobody can change their own pay. It also covers uploading attendance and pairing it against what was verified, and this is where location checking on shift check-in finally gets switched on and enforced.

Item 11 — D22 records a location stamp without enforcing it, and geo-fencing enforcement only arrives in D23. In homecare, location is the only verification available, so unenforced is materially weaker there than in a facility.
As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, section A1 Deliverable Inventory. Reproduced without alteration.

Release 1 comprises thirty-four deliverables: the eleven already scoped to Release 1, nine brought across from Release 2 (six in full, three as thin slices), and fourteen added at revision. Five deliverables defer to a later release and five are descoped from this SOW; those ten are recorded in the scope revision and are not listed here.

The client specification carries no module section for these deliverables. Everything below is Hiresense analysis, not client text.

Built once, used elsewhere

The request-and-approve pattern built here is the same one M15 uses for timesheet approvals. Build it once.

Records this milestone owns

  • WorkforceRequest — one request to change something, with who asked, who approved, and the before and after values.
  • SalaryRevision — a change to what someone is paid, for one person or a batch.
  • SalaryAdvance, SalaryHold — money paid early, or pay stopped and later released.
  • AttendanceUpload — attendance brought in from outside and matched against what the app recorded.
  • GeoFence — a boundary around a site, used to accept or reject a shift check-in.

How records move

  • WorkforceRequest: drafted → submitted → approved or rejected with a reason → applied
  • SalaryHold: requested → approved → held → released
  • Check-in with enforcement on: inside the boundary → accepted. Outside → rejected, or flagged for someone to review.

Not in this milestone

  • Actually paying the money. That is M13.
  • Statutory filings.

Needs to exist first

  • M8 for placements and attendance. M11 for the associate profile. M1 for roles, since approvals depend on who someone is.

What breaks without it

  • M13 payroll reads approved salary changes, advances and holds.

Items — 6 to build · design 8.0pd, build 20.0pd, QA 10.0pd

M12-I1RequestsD23ScreenL · 3.0pdAI HighMediumSHAREDLow
Who uses it: Ops, Fulfilment lead. One place for every change that needs a second person to approve it. The maker-checker engine Part A names.

Screens to build

  1. Requests
    • Lists changes waiting to be approved with what they would change
    • Approves or rejects a request with a reason
    • Stops the person who raised it approving it

Conditions it must satisfy

  • A request cannot be approved by the person who raised it
  • Every approval and rejection records who, when and why (M0-I4)
  • The before and after values are shown so the approver sees what changes
  • This same engine is reused for internal timesheet approval in M15

    • Changes what one associate is paid, from a date
    • Changes what a group of associates are paid in one go
    • Sends the change for approval

Conditions it must satisfy

  • A revision goes through the approval engine before it takes effect
  • A bulk revision produces one request per person, each individually auditable
  • An effective date in the past is either blocked or requires higher approval
  • Payroll in M13 reads only approved revisions

    • Records money paid early and how it will be recovered
    • Stops an associate's pay, and later restarts it
    • Sends each for approval

Conditions it must satisfy

  • An advance records how it will be recovered, not just that it was paid
  • A hold stops the next pay run for that person and is visible before the run
  • Release restores normal pay from the next cycle
  • All three go through the approval engine

M12-I4Attendance uploadD23ScreenL · 3.0pdAI HighMediumDATALow
Who uses it: Ops. Bringing attendance in from outside and matching it against what the app recorded.

Screens to build

  1. Attendance upload
    • Uploads attendance from outside the platform
    • Matches it against the app check-ins and lists what does not match
    • Resolves a mismatch with a reason

Conditions it must satisfy

  • Uploaded attendance is matched against app check-ins and differences are surfaced
  • A mismatch is resolved by a person, not silently overwritten
  • Resolution is audited because it changes what gets invoiced and paid
  • Row-level errors are named and valid rows still load

M12-I5Geo-fence settingsD23ConfigL · 3.0pdAI HighMediumSPECLow
Who uses it: Platform administrator. Switching on the location check that M8 records but does not act on. Part A names this as a later-phase item.

Screens to build

  1. Geo-fence settings
    • Sets a boundary around each site for facility work, and around the visit address for home visits
    • Sets whether a check-in outside the boundary is allowed, warned or blocked
    • Sets the tolerance, since a phone indoors is less accurate than one outside

Conditions it must satisfy

  • With enforcement off, behaviour is exactly as in M8 — location recorded, nothing blocked
  • With enforcement on, a check-in outside the boundary is rejected or flagged per the mode
  • The location field already exists from M8, so switching this on needs no data change
  • A breach report shows attempts outside the boundary
  • The same check runs for both cases; only the reference point differs - the site record for facility work, the visit address on the shift line for home visits
  • Tolerance is set wide enough that a phone indoors does not produce false rejections

M13 · D24 · Months 6–6
Payroll
Paying the shift workers. This connects the platform to a third-party payroll engine — we do not build a payroll calculator ourselves. It sends the inputs the engine needs, configures the pay cycle, produces the PF and ESIC reports and challans, and delivers payslips to the candidate in the app and over WhatsApp. Until this exists, payroll is done by hand, which puts a hard ceiling on how many shift workers Apollo can take on.

As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, section A1 Deliverable Inventory. Reproduced without alteration.

Release 1 comprises thirty-four deliverables: the eleven already scoped to Release 1, nine brought across from Release 2 (six in full, three as thin slices), and fourteen added at revision. Five deliverables defer to a later release and five are descoped from this SOW; those ten are recorded in the scope revision and are not listed here.

The client specification carries no module section for these deliverables. Everything below is Hiresense analysis, not client text.

Built once, used elsewhere

Payslip delivery reuses the messaging layer from M5. The payroll engine sits behind an interface so it can be swapped later, the same pattern as the registry adapters in M1.

Records this milestone owns

  • PayrollCycle — a pay period and its state.
  • PayrollInput — what goes to the engine for one person for one cycle: hours, rate, adjustments.
  • PayrollOutput — what comes back: net pay, deductions, and the payslip document.
  • StatutoryReport — PF and ESIC reports and challans.

How records move

  • PayrollCycle: open → inputs locked → sent to engine → results received → payslips issued → closed
  • PayrollInput: draft → validated → sent. Or rejected by the engine with a reason.

Not in this milestone

  • Calculating pay ourselves. A third-party engine does that. Hirecraft and PayBooks are the candidates.
  • Submitting statutory filings. We produce the reports and challans; someone else files them.

Needs to exist first

  • M8 and M15 for approved timesheets. M11 for statutory forms. M12 for approved salary changes, advances and holds.

What breaks without it

  • Nothing inside the platform. This is the end of the pay side.

Items — 5 to build · design 6.0pd, build 20.0pd, QA 9.0pd

M13-I1Pay runD24ScreenL · 3.0pdAI MediumHighEXTLow
Who uses it: Finance. Running a pay period: open it, lock the inputs, send it, get the results back, close it.

Screens to build

  1. Pay run
    • Lists pay periods and their state
    • Locks the inputs, sends them to the payroll engine and takes the results back
    • Lists associates blocked from the run and why

Conditions it must satisfy

  • A cycle cannot be locked while associates are blocked on incomplete onboarding (M11-I5)
  • Inputs are frozen at lock so the figures sent match the figures recorded
  • A rejected input from the engine is shown with its reason and can be corrected and resent
  • A closed cycle cannot be reopened without an audited override

M14 · D14 (full) · Months 5–6
Demand Prioritisation & Approval
M3 records which recruiter was put on which vacancy, as decided on the morning call. This milestone makes the system do the deciding: work out which vacancies matter most based on how urgent, how valuable and how old they are, suggest which recruiter should take each one based on their current load and past success, and balance work across the team. It also adds an approval step so a vacancy can be checked before it goes live, which M3 deliberately does not have.
Release 24 items15 conditions26.5pd0 high riskOps/Admin
As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, section A1 Deliverable Inventory. Reproduced without alteration.

Release 1 comprises thirty-four deliverables: the eleven already scoped to Release 1, nine brought across from Release 2 (six in full, three as thin slices), and fourteen added at revision. Five deliverables defer to a later release and five are descoped from this SOW; those ten are recorded in the scope revision and are not listed here.

The client specification carries no module section for these deliverables. Everything below is Hiresense analysis, not client text.

Built once, used elsewhere

This changes M3 behaviour rather than adding new screens beside it. The fulfilment board and requisition record gain new fields and actions.

Records this milestone owns

  • PriorityScore — a score per vacancy and why it got that score.
  • AllocationSuggestion — a recommended recruiter, with the reasoning and whether it was accepted.
  • RecruiterCapacity — current load against the agreed norm of 15-16 vacancies.
  • ApprovalRequest — a vacancy waiting to be approved before it goes live.

How records move

  • Requisition gains: created → awaiting approval → approved → open. Or rejected back to the raiser.
  • AllocationSuggestion: generated → accepted or overridden, with a reason

Not in this milestone

  • Forcing an allocation. The system suggests; a person still decides.
  • Forecasting demand.

Needs to exist first

  • M3 for the requisition and allocation records. M9 for the performance data the suggestions are based on.

What breaks without it

  • M9 gains better data on how allocation decisions turn out.

M15 · D22 (full), D25 (full) · Months 6–6
Timesheet Operations & Collections
Two things left out of the thin slices. First, the full timesheet: leave types, overtime rules, and an internal approval route inside Apollo before it goes to the client. Second, the money coming back in: recording payments received, matching them against invoices including part-payments and TDS, an overdue report, automatic reminders and chasing, and credit and debit notes. Together these turn the platform from one that raises invoices into one that tracks whether Apollo actually got paid.
Release 27 items27 conditions44.0pd0 high riskOps/Admin, Client link
As stated by the client
Apollo Platform Build Specification — Part A, section A1 Deliverable Inventory. Reproduced without alteration.

Release 1 comprises thirty-four deliverables: the eleven already scoped to Release 1, nine brought across from Release 2 (six in full, three as thin slices), and fourteen added at revision. Five deliverables defer to a later release and five are descoped from this SOW; those ten are recorded in the scope revision and are not listed here.

The client specification carries no module section for these deliverables. Everything below is Hiresense analysis, not client text.

Built once, used elsewhere

Internal timesheet approval uses the same request-and-approve pattern built in M12. Chasing reminders use the messaging layer from M5.

Records this milestone owns

  • LeaveType, LeaveRecord — categories of absence and who took what.
  • OvertimeRule — when extra hours are paid at a different rate.
  • TimesheetApprovalRoute — who inside Apollo must approve before it reaches the client.
  • Payment, PaymentAllocation — money received, and which invoices it was matched to.
  • CreditNote, DebitNote — corrections to an invoice already raised.
  • DunningSchedule — when and how overdue invoices get chased.

How records move

  • Timesheet gains: built → internally approved → sent to client → client approved → invoiced
  • Invoice: raised → partly paid → fully paid. Or credited.
  • Payment: received → matched to invoices → any remainder left unmatched

Not in this milestone

  • Being the accounting system of record. The finance system stays the book of record; this tracks against it.

Needs to exist first

  • M8 for timesheets. M9 for invoices and receivables. M2 for contract payment terms.

What breaks without it

  • M13 payroll reads the fuller timesheet including leave and overtime.

Integrations

Every outside system the platform touches. Half of them have no named supplier yet. Each unnamed supplier is an unpriced integration and a failure mode nobody can test against.

The client has asked for consumption volumes. Part B asks for message and API volumes per requisition across WhatsApp, email and transcription, so running costs can be modelled. WhatsApp is billed per conversation and the contact schedule drives the volume, so this cannot be answered until the M5 cadence design settles (OI-HS-05). Payroll and voice add further per-unit costs in M13 and M5.

Dependencies

Everything the platform needs from outside the build team, and what specifically is required from Apollo for each. These are separate from the open items, which are decisions about what to build; these are accounts, contracts, registrations and access that must exist before a build can start or a supplier can be called.

Start these in week one. Five things should start in week one regardless of when their milestone begins: Meta business verification for WhatsApp, DLT registration for SMS, the verification aggregator contract, the two app store accounts, and the decision on whether the console federates to Apollo's identity provider. The first four are pure waiting on someone else's timetable and no amount of delivery capacity shortens them. The fifth changes what gets built, so it is cheapest to answer before anything is built at all.
22
Dependencies
Almost all need someone at Apollo, not the delivery team.
13
Needed by month 2
Procurement and registration lead times sit inside this.
3
Suppliers unnamed
Cannot be sized until Apollo names them.
3
Via Apollo’s clients
Two hops. The slowest track.
8
Tracks
Each moves at a different speed.
Most of these are not technical work. They are accounts, contracts, registrations and decisions that have to exist before a build can start or a supplier can be called, and almost all of them need someone at Apollo rather than someone on the delivery team. The items marked as coming through Apollo's clients are the slowest, because they need Apollo to go and ask their own hospital clients, which is two hops rather than one.

X06Corporate single sign-onMonth 1Access & identityApollo IT
First needed for M0-I1 sign in. This changes what M0 builds. Email and password is a small build; federating to a corporate identity provider is a different one, and retrofitting it after ninety users are live is worse than either. Decide before M0 starts.

Required from Apollo

  • Decide whether the Ops and Admin console must federate to Apollo's identity provider
  • If so, name the provider and supply the federation configuration

First needed for M7-I12 link recipients, M9-I14 billing evidence. The day-90 answer must be timestamped against an identified person for the invoice to be defensible in a billing query. Without a recipient model there is no defensible trigger.
